TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

  Washington and New Delhi have more to focus on than Beijing.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s South Asia Brief. The highlights this week: The prospect of more stable U.S.-China relations raises questions for India, Pakistan’s main opposition party postpones a planned protest, and India’s Cockroach movement sets its eyes on a new target. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s South Asia Brief. The highlights this week: The prospect of more stable U.S.-China relations raises questions for India, Pakistan’s main opposition party postpones a planned protest, and India’s Cockroach movement sets its eyes on a new target. Last week, U.S. President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping met at the White House and agreed to lower some tariffs, to partner on energy and trade, and to keep talking. Their summit showed that the rival superpowers are keen to ease bilateral tensions—or at least to better manage them. What would relatively more stable U.S.-China relations mean for ties between Washington and New Delhi? Though a shifting status quo might pose new obstacles, it ultimately wouldn’t be an all-around bad thing for India. To be sure, China is the glue that binds the strategic dimension of the U.S.-India partnership; Washington and New Delhi have long shared the view that Beijing’s growing power constitutes a threat. This has motivated growing U.S.-India defense cooperation and the resurgence of the Quadrilateral Security Dialogue, as well as stepped-up collaborations on technology . With U.S.-India ties currently mired in tensions of their own and a persistent trust gap in New Delhi, undermining the strategic rationale for partnership could present more headaches. However, the U.S.-India relationship is focused on a lot more than China. In recent years, bilateral ties have grown broader and more multifaceted. Trade, educational exchanges, and space are all significant areas of collaboration outside the strategic realm. One recent major achievement was the joint launch of a satellite last year. Despite Russia-driven tensions over fuel, energy has long been a key area of U.S.-India cooperation, going back to the early 1990s , as bilateral ties were beginning to bounce back from a few decades of poor relations—well before the emergence of major competition with China. Reduced U.S.-China tensions could also lessen the overall threat to Indian security interests. Effectively, China might feel less inclined to drag India into its rivalry with the United States. That could translate to China refraining from provocations against India

Iraqis celebrated the United States’ long-awaited withdrawal on Wednesday, as many had blamed Baghdad’s years of instability on the foreign military’s presence. Hundreds of thousands of Iraqi civilians and thousands of U.S. troops were killed during Washington’s two decades of involvement . However, with the Iran war still active across the region, experts warn that the United States’ departure now leaves behind a power vacuum that U.S. adversaries are likely to take advantage of. U.S. troops invaded Iraq in 2003 to oust Sunni dictator Saddam Hussein, and they remained in the country until 2011 to support its Shiite-led government. Less than three years later, though, U.S. forces returned to help fight the Islamic State. Then, in 2024, President Joe Biden struck an agreement with Baghdad to end the U.S. military presence in Iraq by September 2025. However, developments in neighboring Syria led Iraq to allow a small contingent of U.S. advisors and security personnel to remain beyond that deadline. U.S. forces also continued to operate from the country’s semi-autonomous Kurdistan region. In recent months, U.S. forces have carried out military operations against pro-Iranian paramilitary groups in Iraq, which have claimed responsibility for repeated strikes on U.S. troops in the region as well as a recent drone attack on Saudi Arabia’s East-West pipeline. Despite Baghdad remaining the only Middle Eastern government to maintain close military and political ties with both Tehran and Washington, analysts argue that Iraq has become a secondary battlefield in the Iran war; of the 18 U.S. service members killed since the Iran war began in February, seven died ​in Iraq. Wednesday’s withdrawal could exacerbate that instability. “The most important question now is whether Iraq’s security institutions have prepared a viable Plan B for the ​post-coalition phase,” retired Iraqi Army Gen. Mohammed al-Jubouri told Reuters. Locals aren’t optimistic. “We believe Iraq is still not fully ready to take over the security file ​at this time,” Abdulrahman al-Zobaie, a Sun

For many in Latvia, the Baltic nation of just over 1.8 million people, the dangers of Russia’s war in Ukraine are increasingly real. At a Workers’ Party convention in August, Brazilian President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva made an unusual appeal to his own political camp: “I want to ask the left-wing people,” he said. “Stop this nonsense of thinking that we don’t have to worry about defense.” Lula then explained what Brazil had to protect, including nearly 10,500 miles of land borders, the Amazon rainforest, 12 percent of global freshwater, and mineral wealth. Though Lula said the country should promote peace rather than war, his message was clear: Brazil needs armed forces capable of deterring threats. Lula has run seven presidential campaigns , and this is the first to feature defense policy as a key pillar of his platform. This may be surprising, given the close ties between the Brazilian military and influential former right-wing President Jair Bolsonaro. Some military leaders cooperated with Bolsonaro’s failed efforts to overturn Lula’s 2022 victory; Bolsonaro has since been convicted of plotting a coup and barred from politics. His son Flávio Bolsonaro is running in his place ahead of the Oct. 4 first-round vote. Lula’s shift on defense can be explained at least in part by U.S. foreign policy. U.S. President Donald Trump’s new military campaign in Latin America as well as his sanctions and tariffs targeting Brazil have given a new life to the idea that the country’s sovereignty needs to be protected. A longtime backer of industrial policy, Lula has also leaned into the potential of Brazil’s military-industrial complex to boost the economy. During Lula’s previous presidential terms—between 2003 and 2011—he was not hostile to military spending. He advanced some of Brazil’s biggest defense programs, such as a conventional and nuclear submarine partnership with France , the development of Embraer’s KC-390 tactical transport aircraft, and the Guarani armored vehicle initiative.

* How Far Will the Canada-EU Lovefest Go?
  Foreign Policy | 30 Sep 11:53 UTC
  Both Ottawa and Brussels have good reasons to improve ties, but the EU will need to become more flexible.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Can Canada become an associate member of the European Union? Why not? It ticks all the boxes as far as the EU is concerned: a robust democracy, a vibrant economy, and an unflinching commitment to multilateralism, trans-Atlantic security, and the defense of Ukraine. Can Canada become an associate member of the European Union? Why not? It ticks all the boxes as far as the EU is concerned: a robust democracy, a vibrant economy, and an unflinching commitment to multilateralism, trans-Atlantic security, and the defense of Ukraine. Yet Canada won’t join Europe anytime soon, certainly not in a formal way. What both parties will do instead is double down on mutual commitments. Canada and Europe need each other more than ever. With the multilateral global order in tatters and Donald Trump in charge of U.S. foreign policy, they will either hang together or hang apart. The United States is still the partner of choice for Brussels and Ottawa, but both capitals realize they now need to diversify their economic and security relations in order to gain leverage over Washington. Most recently, European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen floated the possibility of Canada’s associate membership in her State of the Union address in September. The trouble is that there is no such thing as associate membership in the EU’s founding treaties. Sure, Article 217 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union talks about “association” with third countries, “involving reciprocal rights and obligations, common action and special procedure.” Agreements based on that clause form the cornerstone of the relationship between the EU and aspiring members, such as the Western Balkan states or Ukraine. But Canada’s own treaty with the EU, the Comprehensive Economic and Trade Agreement (CETA), is tied to an altogether different article that governs the union’s commercial policy in the wider world. Unlike Albania, Montenegro, or Moldova, Canada is not applying for membership and therefore not ready to absorb EU legislation without mutual concessions or adjustments. Von der Leyen has come up with a headlines-spurning slogan. But pity the lawyers in the bowels of European Commission whose job it is to turn it into something legally and bureaucratically workable. In truth, Canada and the EU are already doing a great deal to expand and deepen their partnership and look beyond CETA, which was signed in 2016. During his visit to Brussels in Sep
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/30/canada-european-union-membership-trump-ottowa-brussels/

  The secretary of defense is now the most influential senior official in the Trump administration.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. While I’ve done a decent job of predicting the overall arc of U.S. President Donald Trump’s foreign policy, I’ve also gotten some things profoundly wrong. For example, I—like many in Washington—thought we were headed into a new period of tension and competition with China. While I’ve done a decent job of predicting the overall arc of U.S. President Donald Trump’s foreign policy, I’ve also gotten some things profoundly wrong. For example, I—like many in Washington—thought we were headed into a new period of tension and competition with China. Worse, I told a journalist last year that I didn’t think Trump wanted a war with Iran, a mistake that has prompted me to reconsider not just Trump’s approach to Iran, but my understanding of his decision-making process writ large. But in thinking through predictions about the second Trump administration, I’m struck that practically everybody got Pete Hegseth wrong. Is there a single person who predicted that the secretary of defense would become one of the most influential—potentially even the most effective—senior officials within the Trump administration? * Thailand’s Demographic Crisis Arrives Early
  Foreign Policy | 30 Sep 10:00 UTC
  The country faces a plummeting birth rate sooner than some of its global south peers.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. At the start of the year, Thailand’s Department of Health held a press conference under the ominous title, “Every Birth Matters… When the Silent Countdown Begins.” Officials said that in 2025, deaths in the country had exceeded births by nearly 140,000, and that if current trends held, the population could halve in the next 50 years. A director-general from the department warned that the decline could affect Thailand’s economy, tax base, and even government stability. In a statement in July, the National Savings Fund, which handles public pensions, echoed this alarm. It described declining births as a “warning sign,” as a contracting labor force puts pressure on the welfare system and forces “everyone to rely more on themselves.” Other officials have said that working-aged people will be forced to shoulder a greater burden to support an elderly population, which could lead to an increase in the retirement age. At the start of the year, Thailand’s Department of Health held a press conference under the ominous title, “Every Birth Matters… When the Silent Countdown Begins.” Officials said that in 2025, deaths in the country had exceeded births by nearly 140,000, and that if current trends held, the population could halve in the next 50 years. A director-general from the department warned that the decline could affect Thailand’s economy, tax base, and even government stability. In a statement in July, the National Savings Fund, which handles public pensions, echoed this alarm. It described declining births as a “warning sign,” as a contracting labor force puts pressure on the welfare system and forces “everyone to rely more on themselves.” Other officials have said that working-aged people will be forced to shoulder a greater burden to support an elderly population, which could lead to an increase in the retirement age. Thailand’s sharply declining birth rate follows a pattern often attributed to rising costs of living and women attaining higher levels of education. However, the demographic crisis has arrived in upper-middle-income Thailand early compared to elsewhere in the global south. The country’s fertility rate of 1.2 births per woman places it near Italy, Singapore, Spain, and South Korea—economies with four to 11 times Thailand’s per capita GDP. South Korea’s fertility rate first dropped below 2.1 births per woman—generally seen as replacement level—in 1984, as its economy was growing rapidly. Thailand, by com
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/30/thailand-demographic-crisis-birth-rate-declining-economy/

* Analysis: Wind and solar save UK from gas imports worth £5.9bn during Hormuz crisis
  Carbon Brief | 30 Sep 14:12 UTC
  The UK has avoided the need for gas imports worth £5.9bn since the start of the Hormuz crisis as a… The post Analysis: Wind and solar save UK from gas imports worth £5.9bn during Hormuz crisis appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The UK has avoided the need for gas imports worth £5.9bn since the start of the Hormuz crisis as a result of record generation from wind and solar, reveals Carbon Brief analysis. While gas prices are surging to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis , the UK has been generating record amounts of power from wind and solar, up 14% year-on-year. This unprecedented clean-power generation is directly cutting the need for gas-fired electricity, which is down by nearly 10% year-on-year in 2026 to date. In total, wind and solar have generated a record 41% share of the UK’s electricity needs in 2026 to date, compared with 25% from gas, according to Carbon Brief’s analysis. The figure below shows that wind and solar has avoided the need for UK gas imports worth £5.9bn, since the outbreak of war between the US and Iran in February 2026. These avoided gas imports would have required the UK to secure the equivalent of more than 100 additional tanker deliveries of liquefied natural gas (LNG). The £1.3bn import saving in September 2026 to date is the result of record wind and solar output, at nearly 10 terawatt hours (TWh), combined with surging gas prices. The latest analysis updates figures published by Carbon Brief in April and May . Wholesale gas prices in the UK have remained elevated ever since Russia cut off supplies to Europe in the wake of its invasion of Ukraine in 2022. Gas averaged 90p per therm from 2023 until the start of this year, roughly three times above 2019 prices, before the Covid and Ukraine crises. Since the outbreak of war in the Middle East in March, gas prices have climbed higher still, averaging 134p per therm or nearly four times the level seen in 2019. In September 2026 to date, gas prices have averaged 189p per therm, reaching their highest level since the global energy crisis in 2022, as shown in the figure below. UK gas prices are spiking again because winter is approaching – meaning higher demand for heating – and there is no end in sight for the Hormuz crisis. At the same time, European gas stocks are low . This means Europe will have to compete with Asia to secure the cargoes of LNG needed to keep warm. In the UK, high wholesale gas prices are hitting household gas bills under the price cap set by energy regulator Ofgem, whereas electricity bills have barely increased. From this Thursday, 1 October, typical household gas bills will be 33% higher than they were in April, some £200 per year, according to thinktank Nesta . In contras
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-wind-and-solar-save-uk-from-gas-imports-worth-5-9bn-during-hormuz-crisis

There is something magnetic about decay. It repels and attracts in the same breath. I think it unsettles because it reminds us of what we are made of: flesh that can soften, skin that can host growth, breath that can carry contamination. The strange intimacy in the way the surface gives way and something underneath begins to show makes it difficult to turn away, not because it is unfamiliar, but because it isn’t. One thread of the fraught tensions that exist between Russians and Buryats dates back to the 17th century, when Buryat nomadic tribes faced the arrival of Russian Cossacks colonizing the lands around Lake Baikal. In 1727, the Russian Empire and the Qing dynasty established the Sino-Russian border and cut off Buryats from the rest of the Mongol people, who today live mostly in Mongolia and Southern Mongolia (officially known as Inner Mongolia, in China). The Russian conquest was violent, with documented abuses and forced conversions to Christianity. It was in this borderland, in the village of Tsagan-Chelutai — 130 miles from the junction between Russia, Mongolia and China — that Rabdanov was born in 1994. His father had run a collective farm during the Soviet period and later worked in the district administration. His mother taught English. At home, everyone spoke Buryat. “When I was a toddler, I didn’t know any Russian,” said Rabdanov. That changed at kindergarten, where Russian was the only language allowed. The district was two-thirds Buryat, but it offered no Buryat classes until ninth grade. In his essay “ From the Tree to the Labyrinth ,” Umberto Eco, a lover of labyrinths, explores how we “create and organize knowledge.” Moving away from the restrictive “tree” model, Eco approaches the “labyrinth” model, listing three kinds—a unicursal labyrinth, with only one path to the center and back; a multicursal maze, with many paths, all but one leading to dead ends; and an infinite network of interlinked points, which he calls by the borrowed term “rhizome.” There are many labyrinths and mazes woven into my memory—verbal, textual, graphical, physical, metaphorical. Tamil kolam s , for instance: My mother draws small, perfect threshold patterns at home, and immense, geometrically flawless labyrinths and mazes for events—no outlines, no tools, just her seeing eye and moving hand. When the sky is still dark, she dips folded muslin in wet, ground rice and draws liquid lines on the clean floor. She must draw each bit before the white dries on her hand, which it does almost instantly. Nothing can be erased or redrawn. She must keep moving until the whole kolam is done—the body walking the labyrinth even as it summons it out of nothing, the labyrinth making the world even as it is devoured by little black ants. Eco’s rhizome model, to its credit, says we may never know everything, but we can keep going. Jorge Luis Borges certainly did, over and over like a modern-day Scheherazade. A few weeks later, he’d found himself in an unfamiliar urologist’s office, at the edge of an abyss, ears ringing with what may be three of the most dreaded words in medicine: ‘You have cancer.’ This diagnosis portends pain, suffering and death: in the United States alone, cancer kills more than 600,000 people a year, enough lives to empty a city. Yet, receiving a cancer diagnosis doesn’t necessarily mean what it once did. William was in very broad company: more than 2 million Americans a year receive the diagnosis and, for many, the finding entails little to no threat to length or quality of life, except through the ill effects of treatments. However, patients labelled with the ‘C-word’ face substantial clinical, psychological, social and economic consequences. This disconnect – between what the word evokes in my exam room and what it often describes in contemporary practice – drives controversies and variability in screening practices, and in light of our growing understanding of cellular biology, demands that we rethink what we really mean when we apply the label ‘cancer’. William’s cancer was in his prostate, an organ of whose existence he was previously only vaguely aware, but whose removal or radiation risked impairments to urinary, bowel and sexual function. His biopsy had revealed a prostate tumour we designate ‘grade group 1’, which features few molecular hallmarks of cancer and no immediate potential for metastasis or death. Nonetheless, beset with many competing opinions from his rapidly expanding medical team, William found himself rushing headlong toward treatment, and wrestling with a constant anxiety he’d never felt before. By the time he reached my office, he had taken medical leave from work and confided that, perversely, he was smoking for the first time in decades.   Aeon | 25 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Jürgen Habermas, the great defender of deliberative democracy, lived up to its demands: he never feared changing his mind - by Emilie Prattico Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jürgen Habermas at the philosophical faculty of Frankfurt University, Germany, in January 1969. All photos by Max Scheler/Süddeutsche Zeitung/Alamy Jürgen Habermas died on 14 March 2026, in the German town of Starnberg, aged 96. Every fall, from the mid-1990s into the late-2000s, he would come to Northwestern University in Evanston, Illinois to teach and give talks, a visit that a dedicated group of students looked forward to every fall term. The year that has stayed with me is 2004, when he taught what he’d spelled out in Between Facts and Norms (1992), his opus applying his theory to democratic politics. Law, philosophy, history, political science: students spanned many disciplines to come watch the famous German scholar every week, dressed in Wrangler jeans and a tweed jacket, expose his theory of deliberative politics in his unmistakable nasal voice, thick German accent, and impeccable English. Max Weber and the Frankfurt School, which were intimate to him, since he’d been Theodor Adorno’s research assistant in the 1950s, came to life in his speech and then in schemas and figures in chalk as he theorised about them. I understood almost none of it at first: words I’d read were coming to life in the room, spoken by their author, and still not resolving into sense. After several lectures, the shape came into focus: this wasn’t just Between Facts and Norms being rebuilt in public, but decades of work arriving at a single place. Since The Theory of Communicative Action (1981), Habermas had been arguing that reason survives in modern life only as something people do together, that is, by giving each other reasons and staying open to being moved by them, rather than by force, money or tradition. Between Facts and Norms was that idea turned toward law and democracy directly: a state counts as legitimate only when its laws could in principle be justified to everyone bound by them, through exactly that kind of open exchange. I’d watched that argument take root in a decades-long intellectual history. Thinking is a practice, it involves owing each other explanations, questions, answers. It is not a fast process, and doing it in public, out loud, in front of a room, is what makes it worthwhile. I attended all of Habermas’s seminars during my time at Northwestern with great interest and, to be honest, a sense of awe. One day, the topic of the weekly seminar was inclusion, and I had argued, with the timid conviction of a green graduate student, that deliberative publ

  FULL TEXT: Audiophiles are sometimes described in terms reserved for cults; a 1956 New Yorker story, referenced in Colin Vanderburg’s exceptional tour of extreme listening, refers to “a remarkably articulate group of zealots” and “an almost mystical reverence for sound.” Vanderburg travels among the believers, tracing the decades-long promise of “consumer audio innovations” to their technological apex: the New York International Audio Show, where audiophiles sample six-figure sound systems in pursuit of a signal as pure as the world they already inhabit. More than anything, I have spent long passages of this earthly life sitting on my ass, listening. I listen to music, but what I hear is sound. Texture, timbre, and tone; clarity and detail; space and depth. And I hear, even more acutely, what thwarts sound. Shrill treble, muddled bass — for me these are worse than peeves. They offend hearing. They diminish the human sensorium. Earbuds repulse me; laptop speakers grate; a tabletop Bluetooth unit is a kind of vandalism. The word audiophile — etymologically, one who “loves to hear” — has a wine-sipping pretension that I hate, but sometimes an ugly shoe fits. Like similarly suffixed psychopathologies, the term suggests a state of warped dependence, of inbent sublimation. I didn’t set out to be this way, but neither have I ever sought help. My symptoms are apparent, their causes obscure. What, after all, do we love when we love sound? “The neurophysiological disorder is characterized by a severe aversion to sound—and the struggle to convince others of the severity of that aversion.” “How techno’s most vaunted architect is still building sonic futures.” “A physics expert and audio engineer may have solved the mystery of a strange noise that rattled an Oregon town in 2016.”

The visa restrictions are the latest escalation in a bigger fight over how South Africa’s white minority is represented. A far-right narrative long promoted by Afrikaner interest groups and their American allies is now helping shape U.S. policy toward South Africa: that murders in rural farming communities amount to an organized campaign against white people — rather than one part of the national crime crisis in a country with one of the highest murder rates in the world. Trump has falsely claimed that a “white genocide” is taking place in South Africa. Critics, including Rasool, argue that the Trump administration is using a narrative of white victimhood to advance its own politics at home. Washington is no longer merely amplifying that narrative. It is using it to justify preferential refuge for Afrikaners and threaten visa restrictions against people it holds responsible for policies it describes as discriminatory. The visa restrictions, announced by Rubio on Sept. 15, target “foreign nationals who are responsible for, or complicit in, the enactment or implementation of laws or policies that enable uncompensated land seizures, race-based discrimination, and/or the incitement of imminent violence against members of minority ethnic or racial groups in South Africa.” The State Department announcement says certain family members could also be affected, without providing further detail.   FULL TEXT: R.101 airship parked in Cardington, UK. Image by the Royal Aeronautical Society In this short video, Bill Hammack, an engineer and professor at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, details the extraordinary making and tragic ending of the British airship known as R101. Conceived in 1924 and first flown in 1929, R101 was intended to fly a regular route from England to India. Hammack uses archival images and his own deep research to trace the engineering and history of this remarkable vessel, which the British government planned as the flagship of a fleet that would criss-cross its then-sprawling empire. Those ambitious plans were abandoned after the airship crashed in France in October 1930, killing 48 of the 54 people on board. Hammack offers a riveting peek into a brief moment in aviation history – one ended for good by the crash of the Hindenburg in 1937 – when it appeared as if commercial airships might become a regular sight in skies across the world. The video accompanies Hammack’s book Fatal Flight: The True Story of Britain’s Last Great Airship (2017), which is available as a free audiobook at his website . * Where a land ethic blooms

  Caring for this planet entails decisions that are intimate, about our farms, our homes, our human and our wild neighbours - by Craig Maier Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Managed grazing of beef cattle at the Gasser Farm in Witwen, in the southern Wisconsin Driftless Area, US. Photo by Randy Jackson Forty iron rectangles, each one blunt as a piano key, rotate into a blurring fury that hides the eight-foot-long shaft at the heart of the flail chopper. I am perched atop several tons of iron, with 80 horsepower at my command. This part of farming is straightforward – even a mechanically challenged tree-hugger like me can manage to depress the clutch, shift into second, and ease the throttle forward. The tractor’s tall, deeply treaded tires churn, revolving into a smooth spin. Everything is spinning: fans, belts, alternator, the driveshaft, axles. I don’t understand the mechanism fully, yet the tractor and chopper and I are propelled forward across the knee-high whiskers of a coarsely bearded cornfield. It is Indian summer in southern Wisconsin. My wife and I and our young son live here on her family’s farm. Both of us are employed off the farm, but on weekends and many evenings I am an apprentice to my father-in-law, David. Today, we are taking advantage of a brief period of warm, dry weather. Winter is approaching, and I could be spending the day beside our woodsplitter – the gasoline-sipping, hydraulically heroic mechanism that makes it possible to put up several cords of firewood quickly each year – but that work can wait until the cool, sunny days of fall are shoved aside by the relentless spin of the earth into darkness, sleet, snow and frozen ground. Managed grazing beef farm in the southern Wisconsin Driftless Area, Cavern Point Farm, Marshfield, Wisconsin. Photo by Randy Jackson The wall of tall green corn that stood here this summer has been transformed by fall and harvest. Little is standing after the field was stripped by a seed-combing combine. The mechanical tornado flails so loudly behind me: wailing against the air, grinding off the root-buttressed corn-stalk stumps, sucking up spent leaves and stripped cobs. In my wake, dry corn litter passes through the chopper like water compelled into frothy turbulence, falling again into an even, silent surface that hides so much activity below. No time to waste – the weather is expected to hold for another day, and I need to cover the field before the light fails this afternoon.
